========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main thing is… I’m really struggling to finish things all the way through at work, even when I know exactly what I’m supposed to do. Like, I’ll start the sanitation steps, or I’ll be working on the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’ll get most of it done, but somehow I leave out the last parts. Not because I don’t care—actually it really worries me, because I know my job depends on finishing those things correctly.

It’s embarrassing because these are not optional little tasks. They’re important, and I know that while I’m doing them. But I’ll think I’ve completed everything, and then later realize I missed the final check or didn’t finish the last section or didn’t close something out properly. It keeps happening enough that I’m scared I’m going to get in serious trouble, or that people are going to think I’m careless. That’s basically why I came in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… the main place I notice it is definitely at work. That’s the part that scared me enough to come in, because it’s not like forgetting to finish some random chore. It’s the sanitation steps, or the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork—things where there are supposed to be specific steps, and I’ll start them, I’ll be doing them, I’ll even know they matter, and then somehow the last part just doesn’t get done.

It’s almost like I get most of the way through and my brain acts like it’s finished before it actually is. Like I’ll think, okay, I handled that, and then later there’s this awful moment where I realize I missed the final section or didn’t close out the last check or something like that. And it’s not because I’m trying to cut corners. I’m really not. I know my job depends on doing it correctly, which is why it’s so upsetting.

At home or with personal projects… I guess I’d have to think more about specific examples, but the thing I can point to clearly is the work stuff, because it keeps happening with those final steps. That’s what made me feel like, okay, this isn’t just a one-time mistake.

I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, but honestly the thing that comes to mind most clearly is still the work stuff, because that’s where it’s been the most obvious and where the consequences feel bigger.

Like, I’ll start the sanitation steps, and I know there’s a whole sequence I’m supposed to follow, or I’ll be doing the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’m not confused about what I’m supposed to do. But somehow I’ll get almost all the way through and then leave out the last part, like the final section or the last check or whatever closes it out.

It’s not like I decide to stop. It feels more like I think I’m done before I’m actually done, and then later I realize, oh no, I didn’t finish that last piece. So outside of work, I’m having trouble giving a specific example right now, but the pattern I’m worried about is that follow-through part—starting something important and then somehow not completing the final step.

I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless, like in a big dramatic way. I mean, I get upset and stressed about work stuff, and I can get kind of embarrassed or frustrated with myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as feeling down.\n\nAs far as interest in things… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that I’ve stopped liking things I normally like. It’s more like when I’m overwhelmed I don’t always have the energy to do much, but I don’t think I’ve lost interest exactly. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar difficulties with finishing tasks or missing final steps in other areas of your life, like at home or when working on personal projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the main place I notice it is definitely at work. That’s the part that scared me enough to come in, because it’s not like forgetting to finish some random chore. It’s the sanitation steps, or the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork—things where there are supposed to be specific steps, and I’ll start them, I’ll be doing them, I’ll even know they matter, and then somehow the last part just doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s almost like I get most of the way through and my brain acts like it’s finished before it actually is. Like I’ll think, okay, I handled that, and then later there’s this awful moment where I realize I missed the final section or didn’t close out the last check or something like that. And it’s not because I’m trying to cut corners. I’m really not. I know my job depends on doing it correctly, which is why it’s so upsetting.\n\nAt home or with personal projects… I guess I’d have to think more about specific examples, but the thing I can point to clearly is the work stuff, because it keeps happening with those final steps. That’s what made me feel like, okay, this isn’t just a one-time mistake.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any examples of times you’ve had trouble finishing tasks or missing steps outside of work, like at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, but honestly the thing that comes to mind most clearly is still the work stuff, because that’s where it’s been the most obvious and where the consequences feel bigger.\n\nLike, I’ll start the sanitation steps, and I know there’s a whole sequence I’m supposed to follow, or I’ll be doing the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’m not confused about what I’m supposed to do. I know the steps matter. I know my job depends on it being finished correctly. But somehow I’ll get almost all the way through and then leave out the last part, like the final section or the last check or whatever closes it out.\n\nIt’s not like I decide to stop. It feels more like I think I’m done before I’m actually done, and then later I realize, oh no, I didn’t finish that last piece. So outside of work, I’m having trouble giving a specific example right now, but the pattern I’m worried about is that follow-through part—starting something important and then somehow not completing the final step.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you recall if you’ve had trouble paying attention or staying organized in tasks or activities at home or in your personal life, like managing your schedule, keeping track of belongings, or staying focused while reading or watching something?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"Even when I start a shift trying really hard to stay locked in, after a while the cutting, weighing, and checking labels all blur together and I realize I’ve been staring at the same tray or log sheet without taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, the clearest version of that is still at work, honestly. I know you’re asking about home or personal life, but the way it feels is probably the same kind of attention problem.

Like I’ll come into a shift really trying to be careful and stay locked in, because I know I can’t just zone out around cutting, weighing, checking labels, all of that. And at first I’m really focused. I’ll be telling myself, okay, pay attention, do one step at a time, don’t miss anything. But after a while it’s like everything starts blending together—the trays, the labels, the weights, the log sheets. I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same tray or the same paperwork and I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.

It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or not caring. I’m trying really hard, and then suddenly I sort of come back and think, wait, what was I checking? Did I already do that part or was I about to do it? And then I have to re-orient myself, which is embarrassing, because it’s basic stuff I should be able to stay with. So I guess yes, staying focused is a problem, but the example I can explain best is that sustained attention during the repetitive parts of my shift, where after enough time it all kind of blurs and I stop absorbing what I’m looking at.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… actually, when I think back, I think this was there pretty early. My mom still has some of my old elementary school report cards — I want to say around fourth grade — and they say things like I was bright, or I understood the material, but that I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework.

And I remember that, too. It wasn’t that I couldn’t understand the lessons. A lot of the time I did understand what the teacher was saying, but I would miss pieces of the instructions somehow, especially if there were multiple steps. Like if the teacher explained what we were supposed to do and then walked away, I’d realize partway through that I had missed a chunk of it, or I’d do most of it but not the part that actually made it complete.

If the teacher was nearby or kind of checking in on me, I did better, because then I could be redirected or reminded. But if I was just expected to keep track of it myself, that’s where things would fall apart. It’s kind of embarrassing to say, but it does sound really similar to what happens now — understanding what I’m supposed to do, but still not finishing it or missing the last part unless someone is basically keeping me on track.

But after a while it’s like everything starts blending together—the trays, the labels, the weights, the log sheets. I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same tray or the same paperwork and I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s not like I’m bored on purpose or not caring. I’m trying really hard, and then suddenly I sort of come back and think, wait, what was I checking? Did I already do that part or was I about to do it? And then I have to re-orient myself, which is embarrassing, because it’s basic stuff I should be able to stay with. So I guess yes, staying focused is a problem, but the example I can explain best is that sustained attention during the repetitive parts of my shift, where after enough time it all kind of blurs and I stop absorbing what I’m looking at.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or finishing tasks when you were in school or doing chores as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, when I think back, I think this was there pretty early. My mom still has some of my old elementary school report cards — I want to say around fourth grade — and they say things like I was bright, or I understood the material, but that I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework.\n\nAnd I remember that, too. It wasn’t that I couldn’t understand the lessons. A lot of the time I did understand what the teacher was saying, but I would miss pieces of the instructions somehow, especially if there were multiple steps. Like if the teacher explained what we were supposed to do and then walked away, I’d realize partway through that I had missed a chunk of it, or I’d do most of it but not the part that actually made it complete.\n\nIf the teacher was nearby or kind of checking in on me, I did better, because then I could be redirected or reminded. But if I was just expected to keep track of it myself, that’s where things would fall apart. It’s kind of embarrassing to say, but it does sound really similar to what happens now — understanding what I’m supposed to do, but still not finishing it or missing the last part unless someone is basically keeping me on track.\n\n[Last Question]\nLooking back, were you ever described as restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still during your childhood or teenage years?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 7\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t have a clear memory of being the kid who was always bouncing out of the chair or running around when I wasn’t supposed to. Like, I don’t remember teachers saying I was disruptive in that way, or my parents telling stories about me not being able to sit still.

I might have fidgeted some, but I feel like a lot of kids do, so I don’t know if it stood out. When I think back to school, the things I remember more are not so much restlessness, but… I guess I don’t want to over-answer something I don’t actually know. I just don’t have a strong example of being described as restless or hyperactive when I was younger.

If anything, I’d probably have to ask my mom what she remembers, because she kept more of that stuff than I did. But from my own memory, no, not really anything obvious.

I know you’re asking about home or personal life, but the way it feels is probably the same kind of attention problem.\n\nLike I’ll come into a shift really trying to be careful and stay locked in, because I know I can’t just zone out around cutting, weighing, checking labels, all of that. It wasn’t that I couldn’t understand the lessons. A lot of the time I did understand what the teacher was saying, but I would miss pieces of the instructions somehow, especially if there were multiple steps. But if I was just expected to keep track of it myself, that’s where things would fall apart. I don’t have a clear memory of being the kid who was always bouncing out of the chair or running around when I wasn’t supposed to. Like, I don’t remember teachers saying I was disruptive in that way, or my parents telling stories about me not being able to sit still.\n\nI might have fidgeted some, but I feel like a lot of kids do, so I don’t know if it stood out. When I think back to school, the things I remember more are not so much restlessness, but… I guess I don’t want to over-answer something I don’t actually know. I just don’t have a strong example of being described as restless or hyperactive when I was younger.\n\nIf anything, I’d probably have to ask my mom what she remembers, because she kept more of that stuff than I did. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m honestly not sure. I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a big thing for me, at least not in the way you’re describing it.

Like, I’m trying to think back to school or being at home as a kid, and I don’t remember getting in trouble a lot for blurting out answers or cutting people off, or not waiting my turn in line or games or anything like that. I mean, I’m sure I probably did normal kid stuff sometimes, because most kids do, but I don’t have a strong memory of adults pointing it out as a pattern.

I also don’t remember being seen as the kid who was always interrupting or acting before thinking. If anything, I feel like the things people commented on were different, but for this specific thing—impulsive stuff, blurting things out, waiting turns—I don’t really know. I’d probably have to ask my mom or look back at old comments from teachers to be more certain. From what I remember, though, nothing obvious stands out.

I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar difficulties with finishing tasks or missing final steps in other areas of your life, like at home or when working on personal projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the main place I notice it is definitely at work. It’s the sanitation steps, or the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork—things where there are supposed to be specific steps, and I’ll start them, I’ll be doing them, I’ll even know they matter, and then somehow the last part just doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s almost like I get most of the way through and my brain acts like it’s finished before it actually is. Like I’ll think, okay, I handled that, and then later there’s this awful moment where I realize I missed the final section or didn’t close out the last check or something like that. And it’s not because I’m trying to cut corners. I’m really not. I know my job depends on doing it correctly, which is why it’s so upsetting.\n\nAt home or with personal projects… I guess I’d have to think more about specific examples, but the thing I can point to clearly is the work stuff, because it keeps happening with those final steps. That’s what made me feel like, okay, this isn’t just a one-time mistake.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any examples of times you’ve had trouble finishing tasks or missing steps outside of work, like at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, but honestly the thing that comes to mind most clearly is still the work stuff, because that’s where it’s been the most obvious and where the consequences feel bigger.\n\nLike, I’ll start the sanitation steps, and I know there’s a whole sequence I’m supposed to follow, or I’ll be doing the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’m not confused about what I’m supposed to do. I know the steps matter. I know my job depends on it being finished correctly. But somehow I’ll get almost all the way through and then leave out the last part, like the final section or the last check or whatever closes it out.\n\nIt’s not like I decide to stop. It feels more like I think I’m done before I’m actually done, and then later I realize, oh no, I didn’t finish that last piece. I know you’re asking about home or personal life, but the way it feels is probably the same kind of attention problem.\n\nLike I’ll come into a shift really trying to be careful and stay locked in, because I know I can’t just zone out around cutting, weighing, checking labels, all of that. And at first I’m really focused. I’ll be telling myself, okay, pay attention, do one step at a time, don’t miss anything. But after a while it’s like everything starts blending together—the trays, the labels, the weights, the log sheets. I know the steps matter. I know my job depends on it being finished correctly. But somehow I’ll get almost all the way through and then leave out the last part, like the final section or the last check or whatever closes it out.\n\nIt’s not like I decide to stop. It feels more like I think I’m done before I’m actually done, and then later I realize, oh no, I didn’t finish that last piece. So outside of work, I’m having trouble giving a specific example right now, but the pattern I’m worried about is that follow-through part—starting something important and then somehow not completing the final step.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you’ve had trouble paying attention or staying organized in tasks or activities at home or in your personal life, like managing your schedule, keeping track of belongings, or staying focused while reading or watching something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the clearest version of that is still at work, honestly. I know you’re asking about home or personal life, but the way it feels is probably the same kind of attention problem.\n\nLike I’ll come into a shift really trying to be careful and stay locked in, because I know I can’t just zone out around cutting, weighing, checking labels, all of that. And at first I’m really focused. I’ll be telling myself, okay, pay attention, do one step at a time, don’t miss anything. But after a while it’s like everything starts blending together—the trays, the labels, the weights, the log sheets. I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same tray or the same paperwork and I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s not like I’m bored on purpose or not caring. I’m trying really hard, and then suddenly I sort of come back and think, wait, what was I checking? Did I already do that part or was I about to do it? And then I have to re-orient myself, which is embarrassing, because it’s basic stuff I should be able to stay with. So I guess yes, staying focused is a problem, but the example I can explain best is that sustained attention during the repetitive parts of my shift, where after enough time it all kind of blurs and I stop absorbing what I’m looking at.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or finishing tasks when you were in school or doing chores as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, when I think back, I think this was there pretty early. My mom still has some of my old elementary school report cards — I want to say around fourth grade — and they say things like I was bright, or I understood the material, but that I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework.\n\nAnd I remember that, too. It wasn’t that I couldn’t understand the lessons. A lot of the time I did understand what the teacher was saying, but I would miss pieces of the instructions somehow, especially if there were multiple steps. Like if the teacher explained what we were supposed to do and then walked away, I’d realize partway through that I had missed a chunk of it, or I’d do most of it but not the part that actually made it complete.\n\nIf the teacher was nearby or kind of checking in on me, I did better, because then I could be redirected or reminded. But if I was just expected to keep track of it myself, that’s where things would fall apart. It’s kind of embarrassing to say, but it does sound really similar to what happens now — understanding what I’m supposed to do, but still not finishing it or missing the last part unless someone is basically keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you ever described as restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still during your childhood or teenage years?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t have a clear memory of being the kid who was always bouncing out of the chair or running around when I wasn’t supposed to. Like, I don’t remember teachers saying I was disruptive in that way, or my parents telling stories about me not being able to sit still.\n\nI might have fidgeted some, but I feel like a lot of kids do, so I don’t know if it stood out. When I think back to school, the things I remember more are not so much restlessness, but… I guess I don’t want to over-answer something I don’t actually know. I just don’t have a strong example of being described as restless or hyperactive when I was younger.\n\nIf anything, I’d probably have to ask my mom what she remembers, because she kept more of that stuff than I did. But from my own memory, no, not really anything obvious.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood, were there times when you acted without thinking, blurted things out, or had trouble waiting your turn, even when you knew you should?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a big thing for me, at least not in the way you’re describing it.\n\nLike, I’m trying to think back to school or being at home as a kid, and I don’t remember getting in trouble a lot for blurting out answers or cutting people off, or not waiting my turn in line or games or anything like that. I mean, I’m sure I probably did normal kid stuff sometimes, because most kids do, but I don’t have a strong memory of adults pointing it out as a pattern.\n\nI also don’t remember being seen as the kid who was always interrupting or acting before thinking. If anything, I feel like the things people commented on were different, but for this specific thing—impulsive stuff, blurting things out, waiting turns—I don’t really know. I’d probably have to ask my mom or look back at old comments from teachers to be more certain. From what I remember, though, nothing obvious stands out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do notice myself shifting around sometimes, like crossing and uncrossing my legs or changing position if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t know if I’d call it restlessness in a serious way.

In meetings, I can sit there if I need to. I might be kind of uncomfortable or want to move, but I don’t think people would necessarily look at me and think I can’t stay still. With watching something, it depends—sometimes I sit through it fine, sometimes I move around or get up, but I don’t know if that’s more just normal boredom or being tired.

Reading is harder for me to judge, because if I’m not staying with it, I don’t always know whether it’s because I feel physically restless or something else. I’m not usually thinking, “I have to move right now.” It’s more vague than that. So I guess I haven’t clearly noticed being restless or fidgety as a major adult thing, at least not in a way that stands out to me.

It keeps happening enough that I’m scared I’m going to get in serious trouble, or that people are going to think I’m careless. That’s basically why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless, like in a big dramatic way. I mean, I get upset and stressed about work stuff, and I can get kind of embarrassed or frustrated with myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as feeling down.\n\nAs far as interest in things… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that I’ve stopped liking things I normally like. It’s more like when I’m overwhelmed I don’t always have the energy to do much, but I don’t think I’ve lost interest exactly. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar difficulties with finishing tasks or missing final steps in other areas of your life, like at home or when working on personal projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the main place I notice it is definitely at work. That’s the part that scared me enough to come in, because it’s not like forgetting to finish some random chore. It’s the sanitation steps, or the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork—things where there are supposed to be specific steps, and I’ll start them, I’ll be doing them, I’ll even know they matter, and then somehow the last part just doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s almost like I get most of the way through and my brain acts like it’s finished before it actually is. Like I’ll think, okay, I handled that, and then later there’s this awful moment where I realize I missed the final section or didn’t close out the last check or something like that. And it’s not because I’m trying to cut corners. I’m really not. I know my job depends on doing it correctly, which is why it’s so upsetting.\n\nAt home or with personal projects… I guess I’d have to think more about specific examples, but the thing I can point to clearly is the work stuff, because it keeps happening with those final steps. That’s what made me feel like, okay, this isn’t just a one-time mistake.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any examples of times you’ve had trouble finishing tasks or missing steps outside of work, like at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, but honestly the thing that comes to mind most clearly is still the work stuff, because that’s where it’s been the most obvious and where the consequences feel bigger.\n\nLike, I’ll start the sanitation steps, and I know there’s a whole sequence I’m supposed to follow, or I’ll be doing the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’m not confused about what I’m supposed to do. I know the steps matter. I know my job depends on it being finished correctly. But somehow I’ll get almost all the way through and then leave out the last part, like the final section or the last check or whatever closes it out.\n\nIt’s not like I decide to stop. It feels more like I think I’m done before I’m actually done, and then later I realize, oh no, I didn’t finish that last piece. So outside of work, I’m having trouble giving a specific example right now, but the pattern I’m worried about is that follow-through part—starting something important and then somehow not completing the final step.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you’ve had trouble paying attention or staying organized in tasks or activities at home or in your personal life, like managing your schedule, keeping track of belongings, or staying focused while reading or watching something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the clearest version of that is still at work, honestly. I know you’re asking about home or personal life, but the way it feels is probably the same kind of attention problem.\n\nLike I’ll come into a shift really trying to be careful and stay locked in, because I know I can’t just zone out around cutting, weighing, checking labels, all of that. And at first I’m really focused. I’ll be telling myself, okay, pay attention, do one step at a time, don’t miss anything. But after a while it’s like everything starts blending together—the trays, the labels, the weights, the log sheets. I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same tray or the same paperwork and I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s not like I’m bored on purpose or not caring. I’m trying really hard, and then suddenly I sort of come back and think, wait, what was I checking? Did I already do that part or was I about to do it? And then I have to re-orient myself, which is embarrassing, because it’s basic stuff I should be able to stay with. So I guess yes, staying focused is a problem, but the example I can explain best is that sustained attention during the repetitive parts of my shift, where after enough time it all kind of blurs and I stop absorbing what I’m looking at.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or finishing tasks when you were in school or doing chores as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, when I think back, I think this was there pretty early. My mom still has some of my old elementary school report cards — I want to say around fourth grade — and they say things like I was bright, or I understood the material, but that I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework.\n\nAnd I remember that, too. It wasn’t that I couldn’t understand the lessons. A lot of the time I did understand what the teacher was saying, but I would miss pieces of the instructions somehow, especially if there were multiple steps. Like if the teacher explained what we were supposed to do and then walked away, I’d realize partway through that I had missed a chunk of it, or I’d do most of it but not the part that actually made it complete.\n\nIf the teacher was nearby or kind of checking in on me, I did better, because then I could be redirected or reminded. But if I was just expected to keep track of it myself, that’s where things would fall apart. It’s kind of embarrassing to say, but it does sound really similar to what happens now — understanding what I’m supposed to do, but still not finishing it or missing the last part unless someone is basically keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you ever described as restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still during your childhood or teenage years?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t have a clear memory of being the kid who was always bouncing out of the chair or running around when I wasn’t supposed to. Like, I don’t remember teachers saying I was disruptive in that way, or my parents telling stories about me not being able to sit still.\n\nI might have fidgeted some, but I feel like a lot of kids do, so I don’t know if it stood out. When I think back to school, the things I remember more are not so much restlessness, but… I guess I don’t want to over-answer something I don’t actually know. I just don’t have a strong example of being described as restless or hyperactive when I was younger.\n\nIf anything, I’d probably have to ask my mom what she remembers, because she kept more of that stuff than I did. But from my own memory, no, not really anything obvious.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood, were there times when you acted without thinking, blurted things out, or had trouble waiting your turn, even when you knew you should?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a big thing for me, at least not in the way you’re describing it.\n\nLike, I’m trying to think back to school or being at home as a kid, and I don’t remember getting in trouble a lot for blurting out answers or cutting people off, or not waiting my turn in line or games or anything like that. I mean, I’m sure I probably did normal kid stuff sometimes, because most kids do, but I don’t have a strong memory of adults pointing it out as a pattern.\n\nI also don’t remember being seen as the kid who was always interrupting or acting before thinking. If anything, I feel like the things people commented on were different, but for this specific thing—impulsive stuff, blurting things out, waiting turns—I don’t really know. I’d probably have to ask my mom or look back at old comments from teachers to be more certain. From what I remember, though, nothing obvious stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad most of the time, even when things weren’t stressful at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve definitely had bad days, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as feeling down or sad most of the time, especially separate from work stress.\n\nWhen things aren’t stressful at work, I don’t usually notice myself being sad for no reason, if that makes sense. I might feel tired or kind of worn out sometimes, but “sad most of the day” doesn’t immediately feel like the right description. I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes I don’t label my mood very clearly in the moment.\n\nSo… I guess from what I can tell, no, not really in a consistent way. Not something that stands out to me as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, have you ever noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble staying still for long periods, like during meetings, while reading, or watching something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do notice myself shifting around sometimes, like crossing and uncrossing my legs or changing position if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t know if I’d call it restlessness in a serious way.\n\nIn meetings, I can sit there if I need to. I might be kind of uncomfortable or want to move, but I don’t think people would necessarily look at me and think I can’t stay still. With watching something, it depends—sometimes I sit through it fine, sometimes I move around or get up, but I don’t know if that’s more just normal boredom or being tired.\n\nReading is harder for me to judge, because if I’m not staying with it, I don’t always know whether it’s because I feel physically restless or something else. I’m not usually thinking, “I have to move right now.” It’s more vague than that. So I guess I haven’t clearly noticed being restless or fidgety as a major adult thing, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood, were there times when you acted without thinking, blurted things out, or had trouble waiting your turn, even when you knew you should?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a big thing for me, at least not in the way you’re describing it.\n\nLike, I’m trying to think back to school or being at home as a kid, and I don’t remember getting in trouble a lot for blurting out answers or cutting people off, or not waiting my turn in line or games or anything like that. I mean, I’m sure I probably did normal kid stuff sometimes, because most kids do, but I don’t have a strong memory of adults pointing it out as a pattern.\n\nI also don’t remember being seen as the kid who was always interrupting or acting before thinking. If anything, I feel like the things people commented on were different, but for this specific thing—impulsive stuff, blurting things out, waiting turns—I don’t really know. I’d probably have to ask my mom or look back at old comments from teachers to be more certain. From what I remember, though, nothing obvious stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad most of the time, even when things weren’t stressful at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve definitely had bad days, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as feeling down or sad most of the time, especially separate from work stress.\n\nWhen things aren’t stressful at work, I don’t usually notice myself being sad for no reason, if that makes sense. I might feel tired or kind of worn out sometimes, but “sad most of the day” doesn’t immediately feel like the right description. I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes I don’t label my mood very clearly in the moment.\n\nSo… I guess from what I can tell, no, not really in a consistent way. Not something that stands out to me as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, have you ever noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble staying still for long periods, like during meetings, while reading, or watching something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do notice myself shifting around sometimes, like crossing and uncrossing my legs or changing position if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t know if I’d call it restlessness in a serious way.\n\nIn meetings, I can sit there if I need to. I might be kind of uncomfortable or want to move, but I don’t think people would necessarily look at me and think I can’t stay still. With watching something, it depends—sometimes I sit through it fine, sometimes I move around or get up, but I don’t know if that’s more just normal boredom or being tired.\n\nReading is harder for me to judge, because if I’m not staying with it, I don’t always know whether it’s because I feel physically restless or something else. I’m not usually thinking, “I have to move right now.” It’s more vague than that. So I guess I haven’t clearly noticed being restless or fidgety as a major adult thing, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had times when you felt down or emotionally low even when things were going well in your life, like when work wasn’t stressful or other things were going smoothly?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really think so, at least not in a way that I can clearly point to.

Like, I’ve had times where I’m tired or kind of flat, or I don’t feel super excited about things, but when you say “emotionally low even when things are going well,” I’m not immediately thinking of a period where I was just sad despite everything being fine. I feel like if I’m upset, I can usually connect it to something, even if it’s small or maybe I only realize it later.

I guess I’m also not always great at tracking my mood in the moment, so it’s possible there are times I’d describe differently if I really went back through them. But off the top of my head, no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If work is calm and other things are okay, I don’t usually feel down for no clear reason.

Can you tell me what’s been troubling you enough to come in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is… I’m really struggling to finish things all the way through at work, even when I know exactly what I’m supposed to do. Like, I’ll start the sanitation steps, or I’ll be working on the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’ll get most of it done, but somehow I leave out the last parts. Not because I don’t care—actually it really worries me, because I know my job depends on finishing those things correctly.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because these are not optional little tasks. They’re important, and I know that while I’m doing them. But I’ll think I’ve completed everything, and then later realize I missed the final check or didn’t finish the last section or didn’t close something out properly. It keeps happening enough that I’m scared I’m going to get in serious trouble, or that people are going to think I’m careless. That’s basically why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless, like in a big dramatic way. I mean, I get upset and stressed about work stuff, and I can get kind of embarrassed or frustrated with myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as feeling down.\n\nAs far as interest in things… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that I’ve stopped liking things I normally like. It’s more like when I’m overwhelmed I don’t always have the energy to do much, but I don’t think I’ve lost interest exactly. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar difficulties with finishing tasks or missing final steps in other areas of your life, like at home or when working on personal projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the main place I notice it is definitely at work. That’s the part that scared me enough to come in, because it’s not like forgetting to finish some random chore. It’s the sanitation steps, or the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork—things where there are supposed to be specific steps, and I’ll start them, I’ll be doing them, I’ll even know they matter, and then somehow the last part just doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s almost like I get most of the way through and my brain acts like it’s finished before it actually is. Like I’ll think, okay, I handled that, and then later there’s this awful moment where I realize I missed the final section or didn’t close out the last check or something like that. And it’s not because I’m trying to cut corners. I’m really not. I know my job depends on doing it correctly, which is why it’s so upsetting.\n\nAt home or with personal projects… I guess I’d have to think more about specific examples, but the thing I can point to clearly is the work stuff, because it keeps happening with those final steps. That’s what made me feel like, okay, this isn’t just a one-time mistake.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any examples of times you’ve had trouble finishing tasks or missing steps outside of work, like at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, but honestly the thing that comes to mind most clearly is still the work stuff, because that’s where it’s been the most obvious and where the consequences feel bigger.\n\nLike, I’ll start the sanitation steps, and I know there’s a whole sequence I’m supposed to follow, or I’ll be doing the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’m not confused about what I’m supposed to do. I know the steps matter. I know my job depends on it being finished correctly. But somehow I’ll get almost all the way through and then leave out the last part, like the final section or the last check or whatever closes it out.\n\nIt’s not like I decide to stop. It feels more like I think I’m done before I’m actually done, and then later I realize, oh no, I didn’t finish that last piece. So outside of work, I’m having trouble giving a specific example right now, but the pattern I’m worried about is that follow-through part—starting something important and then somehow not completing the final step.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you’ve had trouble paying attention or staying organized in tasks or activities at home or in your personal life, like managing your schedule, keeping track of belongings, or staying focused while reading or watching something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the clearest version of that is still at work, honestly. I know you’re asking about home or personal life, but the way it feels is probably the same kind of attention problem.\n\nLike I’ll come into a shift really trying to be careful and stay locked in, because I know I can’t just zone out around cutting, weighing, checking labels, all of that. And at first I’m really focused. I’ll be telling myself, okay, pay attention, do one step at a time, don’t miss anything. But after a while it’s like everything starts blending together—the trays, the labels, the weights, the log sheets. I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same tray or the same paperwork and I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s not like I’m bored on purpose or not caring. I’m trying really hard, and then suddenly I sort of come back and think, wait, what was I checking? Did I already do that part or was I about to do it? And then I have to re-orient myself, which is embarrassing, because it’s basic stuff I should be able to stay with. So I guess yes, staying focused is a problem, but the example I can explain best is that sustained attention during the repetitive parts of my shift, where after enough time it all kind of blurs and I stop absorbing what I’m looking at.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or finishing tasks when you were in school or doing chores as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, when I think back, I think this was there pretty early. My mom still has some of my old elementary school report cards — I want to say around fourth grade — and they say things like I was bright, or I understood the material, but that I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework.\n\nAnd I remember that, too. It wasn’t that I couldn’t understand the lessons. A lot of the time I did understand what the teacher was saying, but I would miss pieces of the instructions somehow, especially if there were multiple steps. Like if the teacher explained what we were supposed to do and then walked away, I’d realize partway through that I had missed a chunk of it, or I’d do most of it but not the part that actually made it complete.\n\nIf the teacher was nearby or kind of checking in on me, I did better, because then I could be redirected or reminded. But if I was just expected to keep track of it myself, that’s where things would fall apart. It’s kind of embarrassing to say, but it does sound really similar to what happens now — understanding what I’m supposed to do, but still not finishing it or missing the last part unless someone is basically keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you ever described as restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still during your childhood or teenage years?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t have a clear memory of being the kid who was always bouncing out of the chair or running around when I wasn’t supposed to. Like, I don’t remember teachers saying I was disruptive in that way, or my parents telling stories about me not being able to sit still.\n\nI might have fidgeted some, but I feel like a lot of kids do, so I don’t know if it stood out. When I think back to school, the things I remember more are not so much restlessness, but… I guess I don’t want to over-answer something I don’t actually know. I just don’t have a strong example of being described as restless or hyperactive when I was younger.\n\nIf anything, I’d probably have to ask my mom what she remembers, because she kept more of that stuff than I did. But from my own memory, no, not really anything obvious.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood, were there times when you acted without thinking, blurted things out, or had trouble waiting your turn, even when you knew you should?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I’m not sure I’d say “extremely anxious,” no. I might be kind of uncomfortable or want to move, but I don’t think people would necessarily look at me and think I can’t stay still. I’m not usually thinking, “I have to move right now.” It’s more vague than that. So I guess I haven’t clearly noticed being restless or fidgety as a major adult thing, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt down or emotionally low even when things were going well in your life, like when work wasn’t stressful or other things were going smoothly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, at least not in a way that I can clearly point to.\n\nLike, I’ve had times where I’m tired or kind of flat, or I don’t feel super excited about things, but when you say “emotionally low even when things are going well,” I’m not immediately thinking of a period where I was just sad despite everything being fine. I feel like if I’m upset, I can usually connect it to something, even if it’s small or maybe I only realize it later.\n\nI guess I’m also not always great at tracking my mood in the moment, so it’s possible there are times I’d describe differently if I really went back through them. If I’m stressed, it’s more in the moment, like irritated with myself or worried I’ll have to go back and fix something.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it bothers me, but I don’t think it becomes that intense or that all-consuming most of the time.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave there ever been times when you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual for several days in a row, where it was noticeable to others?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had days where I’m in a better mood or I get more done than usual, and sometimes if I’m excited about something I might talk more, but I’m not thinking of a stretch of several days where I was unusually energetic in a way that other people commented on or that felt really out of character.

The sleep part especially—I don’t really remember times where I needed less sleep and still felt totally fine or energized for days. If I sleep less, I usually feel it. I might push through, but it doesn’t feel like I suddenly don’t need sleep.

And with being more talkative, I can be chatty sometimes depending on who I’m around or what’s going on, but I wouldn’t say there have been periods where people were like, “You’re acting really different,” or anything like that. So, no, not that I’ve noticed.

Can you tell me what’s been troubling you enough to come in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is… I’m really struggling to finish things all the way through at work, even when I know exactly what I’m supposed to do. Like, I’ll start the sanitation steps, or I’ll be working on the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’ll get most of it done, but somehow I leave out the last parts. Not because I don’t care—actually it really worries me, because I know my job depends on finishing those things correctly.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because these are not optional little tasks. They’re important, and I know that while I’m doing them. But I’ll think I’ve completed everything, and then later realize I missed the final check or didn’t finish the last section or didn’t close something out properly. It keeps happening enough that I’m scared I’m going to get in serious trouble, or that people are going to think I’m careless. That’s basically why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I feel hopeless, like in a big dramatic way. I mean, I get upset and stressed about work stuff, and I can get kind of embarrassed or frustrated with myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as feeling down.\n\nAs far as interest in things… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that I’ve stopped liking things I normally like. It’s more like when I’m overwhelmed I don’t always have the energy to do much, but I don’t think I’ve lost interest exactly. I’m not sure if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar difficulties with finishing tasks or missing final steps in other areas of your life, like at home or when working on personal projects?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the main place I notice it is definitely at work. That’s the part that scared me enough to come in, because it’s not like forgetting to finish some random chore. It’s the sanitation steps, or the batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork—things where there are supposed to be specific steps, and I’ll start them, I’ll be doing them, I’ll even know they matter, and then somehow the last part just doesn’t get done.\n\nIt’s almost like I get most of the way through and my brain acts like it’s finished before it actually is. Like I’ll think, okay, I handled that, and then later there’s this awful moment where I realize I missed the final section or didn’t close out the last check or something like that. And it’s not because I’m trying to cut corners. I’m really not. I know my job depends on doing it correctly, which is why it’s so upsetting.\n\nAt home or with personal projects… I guess I’d have to think more about specific examples, but the thing I can point to clearly is the work stuff, because it keeps happening with those final steps. That’s what made me feel like, okay, this isn’t just a one-time mistake.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any examples of times you’ve had trouble finishing tasks or missing steps outside of work, like at home or in your personal life?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of a good example outside of work, but honestly the thing that comes to mind most clearly is still the work stuff, because that’s where it’s been the most obvious and where the consequences feel bigger.\n\nLike, I’ll start the sanitation steps, and I know there’s a whole sequence I’m supposed to follow, or I’ll be doing the batch logs or the end-of-line paperwork, and I’m not confused about what I’m supposed to do. I know the steps matter. I know my job depends on it being finished correctly. But somehow I’ll get almost all the way through and then leave out the last part, like the final section or the last check or whatever closes it out.\n\nIt’s not like I decide to stop. It feels more like I think I’m done before I’m actually done, and then later I realize, oh no, I didn’t finish that last piece. So outside of work, I’m having trouble giving a specific example right now, but the pattern I’m worried about is that follow-through part—starting something important and then somehow not completing the final step.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall if you’ve had trouble paying attention or staying organized in tasks or activities at home or in your personal life, like managing your schedule, keeping track of belongings, or staying focused while reading or watching something?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, the clearest version of that is still at work, honestly. I know you’re asking about home or personal life, but the way it feels is probably the same kind of attention problem.\n\nLike I’ll come into a shift really trying to be careful and stay locked in, because I know I can’t just zone out around cutting, weighing, checking labels, all of that. And at first I’m really focused. I’ll be telling myself, okay, pay attention, do one step at a time, don’t miss anything. But after a while it’s like everything starts blending together—the trays, the labels, the weights, the log sheets. I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same tray or the same paperwork and I’m not actually taking in what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nIt’s not like I’m bored on purpose or not caring. I’m trying really hard, and then suddenly I sort of come back and think, wait, what was I checking? Did I already do that part or was I about to do it? And then I have to re-orient myself, which is embarrassing, because it’s basic stuff I should be able to stay with. So I guess yes, staying focused is a problem, but the example I can explain best is that sustained attention during the repetitive parts of my shift, where after enough time it all kind of blurs and I stop absorbing what I’m looking at.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you have similar issues with staying focused or finishing tasks when you were in school or doing chores as a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah… actually, when I think back, I think this was there pretty early. My mom still has some of my old elementary school report cards — I want to say around fourth grade — and they say things like I was bright, or I understood the material, but that I needed constant reminders to finish my classwork and turn in homework.\n\nAnd I remember that, too. It wasn’t that I couldn’t understand the lessons. A lot of the time I did understand what the teacher was saying, but I would miss pieces of the instructions somehow, especially if there were multiple steps. Like if the teacher explained what we were supposed to do and then walked away, I’d realize partway through that I had missed a chunk of it, or I’d do most of it but not the part that actually made it complete.\n\nIf the teacher was nearby or kind of checking in on me, I did better, because then I could be redirected or reminded. But if I was just expected to keep track of it myself, that’s where things would fall apart. It’s kind of embarrassing to say, but it does sound really similar to what happens now — understanding what I’m supposed to do, but still not finishing it or missing the last part unless someone is basically keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, were you ever described as restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still during your childhood or teenage years?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t have a clear memory of being the kid who was always bouncing out of the chair or running around when I wasn’t supposed to. Like, I don’t remember teachers saying I was disruptive in that way, or my parents telling stories about me not being able to sit still.\n\nI might have fidgeted some, but I feel like a lot of kids do, so I don’t know if it stood out. When I think back to school, the things I remember more are not so much restlessness, but… I guess I don’t want to over-answer something I don’t actually know. I just don’t have a strong example of being described as restless or hyperactive when I was younger.\n\nIf anything, I’d probably have to ask my mom what she remembers, because she kept more of that stuff than I did. But from my own memory, no, not really anything obvious.\n\nDoctor: Looking back at your childhood, were there times when you acted without thinking, blurted things out, or had trouble waiting your turn, even when you knew you should?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a big thing for me, at least not in the way you’re describing it.\n\nLike, I’m trying to think back to school or being at home as a kid, and I don’t remember getting in trouble a lot for blurting out answers or cutting people off, or not waiting my turn in line or games or anything like that. I mean, I’m sure I probably did normal kid stuff sometimes, because most kids do, but I don’t have a strong memory of adults pointing it out as a pattern.\n\nI also don’t remember being seen as the kid who was always interrupting or acting before thinking. If anything, I feel like the things people commented on were different, but for this specific thing—impulsive stuff, blurting things out, waiting turns—I don’t really know. I’d probably have to ask my mom or look back at old comments from teachers to be more certain. From what I remember, though, nothing obvious stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad most of the time, even when things weren’t stressful at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve definitely had bad days, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as feeling down or sad most of the time, especially separate from work stress.\n\nWhen things aren’t stressful at work, I don’t usually notice myself being sad for no reason, if that makes sense. I might feel tired or kind of worn out sometimes, but “sad most of the day” doesn’t immediately feel like the right description. I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes I don’t label my mood very clearly in the moment.\n\nSo… I guess from what I can tell, no, not really in a consistent way. Not something that stands out to me as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, have you ever noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble staying still for long periods, like during meetings, while reading, or watching something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do notice myself shifting around sometimes, like crossing and uncrossing my legs or changing position if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t know if I’d call it restlessness in a serious way.\n\nIn meetings, I can sit there if I need to. I might be kind of uncomfortable or want to move, but I don’t think people would necessarily look at me and think I can’t stay still. With watching something, it depends—sometimes I sit through it fine, sometimes I move around or get up, but I don’t know if that’s more just normal boredom or being tired.\n\nReading is harder for me to judge, because if I’m not staying with it, I don’t always know whether it’s because I feel physically restless or something else. I’m not usually thinking, “I have to move right now.” It’s more vague than that. So I guess I haven’t clearly noticed being restless or fidgety as a major adult thing, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt down or emotionally low even when things were going well in your life, like when work wasn’t stressful or other things were going smoothly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, at least not in a way that I can clearly point to.\n\nLike, I’ve had times where I’m tired or kind of flat, or I don’t feel super excited about things, but when you say “emotionally low even when things are going well,” I’m not immediately thinking of a period where I was just sad despite everything being fine. I feel like if I’m upset, I can usually connect it to something, even if it’s small or maybe I only realize it later.\n\nI guess I’m also not always great at tracking my mood in the moment, so it’s possible there are times I’d describe differently if I really went back through them. But off the top of my head, no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If work is calm and other things are okay, I don’t usually feel down for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel extremely anxious or stressed about missing these final steps, to the point where it’s hard to focus on anything else or it affects your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “extremely anxious,” no. I do get stressed when I realize I’ve missed some final step or I’m worried I might have, because it’s like this annoying little thought that keeps popping back up, like, “Did I actually finish that? Did I leave something undone?” But I don’t know that it takes over everything to the point where I can’t focus on anything else.\n\nSometimes it makes it harder to settle into the next thing, because I’ll keep mentally checking or trying to remember what I did, and that can be frustrating. If I’m stressed, it’s more in the moment, like irritated with myself or worried I’ll have to go back and fix something.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it bothers me, but I don’t think it becomes that intense or that all-consuming most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual for several days in a row, where it was noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. From what I remember, though, nothing obvious stands out.\n\nDoctor: Have you had days where you felt down or sad most of the time, even when things weren’t stressful at work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean, I’ve definitely had bad days, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as feeling down or sad most of the time, especially separate from work stress.\n\nWhen things aren’t stressful at work, I don’t usually notice myself being sad for no reason, if that makes sense. I might feel tired or kind of worn out sometimes, but “sad most of the day” doesn’t immediately feel like the right description. I’d have to think about it more, because sometimes I don’t label my mood very clearly in the moment.\n\nSo… I guess from what I can tell, no, not really in a consistent way. Not something that stands out to me as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, have you ever noticed yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble staying still for long periods, like during meetings, while reading, or watching something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do notice myself shifting around sometimes, like crossing and uncrossing my legs or changing position if I’m sitting for a while, but I don’t know if I’d call it restlessness in a serious way.\n\nIn meetings, I can sit there if I need to. I might be kind of uncomfortable or want to move, but I don’t think people would necessarily look at me and think I can’t stay still. With watching something, it depends—sometimes I sit through it fine, sometimes I move around or get up, but I don’t know if that’s more just normal boredom or being tired.\n\nReading is harder for me to judge, because if I’m not staying with it, I don’t always know whether it’s because I feel physically restless or something else. I’m not usually thinking, “I have to move right now.” It’s more vague than that. So I guess I haven’t clearly noticed being restless or fidgety as a major adult thing, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had times when you felt down or emotionally low even when things were going well in your life, like when work wasn’t stressful or other things were going smoothly?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so, at least not in a way that I can clearly point to.\n\nLike, I’ve had times where I’m tired or kind of flat, or I don’t feel super excited about things, but when you say “emotionally low even when things are going well,” I’m not immediately thinking of a period where I was just sad despite everything being fine. I feel like if I’m upset, I can usually connect it to something, even if it’s small or maybe I only realize it later.\n\nI guess I’m also not always great at tracking my mood in the moment, so it’s possible there are times I’d describe differently if I really went back through them. But off the top of my head, no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If work is calm and other things are okay, I don’t usually feel down for no clear reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel extremely anxious or stressed about missing these final steps, to the point where it’s hard to focus on anything else or it affects your sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “extremely anxious,” no. I do get stressed when I realize I’ve missed some final step or I’m worried I might have, because it’s like this annoying little thought that keeps popping back up, like, “Did I actually finish that? Did I leave something undone?” But I don’t know that it takes over everything to the point where I can’t focus on anything else.\n\nSometimes it makes it harder to settle into the next thing, because I’ll keep mentally checking or trying to remember what I did, and that can be frustrating. But affecting my sleep… I don’t think so, at least not in a clear pattern. I’m not usually lying awake for hours specifically because I missed a final step. If I’m stressed, it’s more in the moment, like irritated with myself or worried I’ll have to go back and fix something.\n\nSo I guess I’d say it bothers me, but I don’t think it becomes that intense or that all-consuming most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have there ever been times when you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep, or were more talkative than usual for several days in a row, where it was noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had days where I’m in a better mood or I get more done than usual, and sometimes if I’m excited about something I might talk more, but I’m not thinking of a stretch of several days where I was unusually energetic in a way that other people commented on or that felt really out of character.\n\nThe sleep part especially—I don’t really remember times where I needed less sleep and still felt totally fine or energized for days. If I sleep less, I usually feel it. I might push through, but it doesn’t feel like I suddenly don’t need sleep.\n\nAnd with being more talkative, I can be chatty sometimes depending on who I’m around or what’s going on, but I wouldn’t say there have been periods where people were like, “You’re acting really different,” or anything like that. So, no, not that I’ve noticed."
